NAVAJO COUNTY BOARD OF SUPERVISORS 
Fern Benally • Alberto L. Peshlakai • Jason E. Whiting • Daryl Seymore • Dawnafe Whitesinger
“We are Navajo County”
 
NOTICE OF PUBLIC HEARING AND AGENDA
Tuesday, May 14, 2024 **Amended Agenda
NAVAJO COUNTY GOVERNMENTAL COMPLEX
BOARD OF SUPERVISORS' CHAMBERS
100 EAST CODE TALKERS DRIVE
HOLBROOK, AZ 86025
One or more supervisors may attend telephonically
Pursuant to A.R.S. §38-431.02(H), the public body will have physical access to the meeting place one (1) hour prior to the start of
the meeting. The Board may vote to meet in a closed executive session to discuss certain matters and for legal advice on any item
as indicated on the following agenda, pursuant to A.R.S. § 38-431.03(A)(3). Items on the agenda may be considered out of order at
the Chairperson's discretion. A copy of the agenda background material provided to the Board, except items to be considered in the
executive session, is available for public inspection at the Navajo County website, www.navajocountyaz.gov 
WATCH THE MEETING LIVE AT: www.navajocountyaz.gov/660
All public comments will need to be made in person or in writing. Written comments will be received by the Clerk of the Board's
office twenty-four (24) hours prior to the Board meeting, at melissa.buckley@navajocountyaz.gov. If further accommodation is
needed, please contact the Clerk of the Board's office, at (928) 524-4053 twenty-four (24) hours prior to the Board Meeting, so
accommodation can be arranged.  
Persons with disabilities who need accommodation to attend or participate in the meeting may contact the Clerk of the Board’s
office at least forty-eight (48) hours prior to the meeting, so accommodation can be arranged.
“NOTICE TO PARENTS AND LEGAL GUARDIANS: Parents and legal guardians have the right to consent before Navajo County makes a video or voice recording
of a minor child, pursuant to A.R.S. § 1-602(A)(9). The Navajo County Board of Supervisors' regular meetings are recorded and may be viewed on Navajo County’s
website and social media pages. If you permit your child to attend/participate in a televised Navajo County Board of Supervisors meeting, a recording will be made, and
your child’s picture may be posted on Navajo County’s social media pages. You may exercise your right not to consent by not allowing your child to attend/participate
in the meeting.”

comments but may respond to criticism, ask staff to review a matter or ask that a matter be
placed on a future agenda.

NAVAJO COUNTY BOARD OF SUPERVISORS’ MINUTES
Tuesday, April 23, 2024
Present: 
Fern Benally, Supervisor District I; Alberto Peshlakai, Chairman; Jason
Whiting, Vice-Chairman; Daryl Seymore, Supervisor District IV;
Dawnafe Whitesinger, Supervisor District V 
Staff
Attendance:
Bryan Layton, County Manager; Brad Carlyon, County Attorney; Jason
Moore, Deputy County Attorney; Melissa Buckley, Clerk of the Board 
9:02 a.m. Call To Order: Invocation and Pledge of Allegiance was led by
Supervisor Alberto Peshlakai.
CALL TO THE PUBLIC:
ReNae Rowles, 400 Freedom Lane, Taylor, talked about cleaning up the voter rolls,
indicating that between December and March she submitted over three hundred names
of voters who had not voted in the past ten years.  She stated that Navajo County is
attempting to maintain their voter rolls properly but that the processes are inadequate to
identify and remove records involving data errors, name changes or confirming that
voters have died.  She talked about the different categories where names were
removed, including in-state duplication registrations, voter registrations in another state,
and deceased voters.  She stated that the county relies on return mail to indicated
voters no longer live at the address, indicating some voters don’t receive the mail and
that mail is often thrown away instead of returned.  She recommended that the county
obtain a legal option on whether they can implement a process for defaulting voters to
inactive unless they contact the county to notify them that the voter is still living at the
registration address.  She talked about a report given to the Recorder and Election
Departments regarding significant gaps in the vulnerable maintenance process and that
since that time many records have been removed due to research by citizens who have
reported the issues to the county.  She stated that this is not a sustainable approach to
correcting the records and that they need the county to advocate for changes in the
system, so they don’t continue to add faulty records with no viable way to remove them. 
She talked about a Supreme Court ruling from 2018 where a voter status could be
changed to inactive in the voter did not reply to a confirmation letter or postcard after
failing to vote in several election cycles.  She stated that the National Voter Registration
Act requires states to conduct a general program that makes a reasonable effort to
remove names of voters who are ineligible due to death or change in residence and
indicated that the county’s current system doesn’t appear to be working.  She requested
that the county review the information that has been provided and to petition the
Legislature and Secretary of State to implement a more proactive approach to voter roll
maintenance so that the registration can be cleaned up. 
Lowry Flake, 202 N. 3rd St. W., Snowflake, talked about the Constitution, indicating that
the Bill of Rights is a key part of the Constitution.  He further talked about the
First Amendment and the Fifth Amendment.  He stated that they depend on the Board to
make sure that the property taken is used on behalf of the public and in their interest
and suggested that they serve without compensation.  
James Parisho, 7697 Sundance Rd., Sun Valley, stated that he is the Chair of the
Navajo County Libertarian Party and area Assistant for the People’s Rights Network.  He
talked about the integrity of elections.  He talked about the county reaching out to the
1 of 12 
DRAFT

<!-- page 9 -->
Republican and Democratic parties about a month ago.  He stated that there is a third
Party in Navajo County, that there are a large number of Libertarians, and that they
deserve equal representation before the Board.  He indicated that currently the
Libertarians do not have ballot access in the county, and that unlike the other two
parties they are required to get approximately fifteen hundred signatures every election
cycle to have their candidates on the ballot, which is why there has never been a
libertarian candidate in Navajo County.  He requested that all parties have equal
representation.  He requested that Navajo County reach out to hm for anything involving
party leadership, indicating that there are more than two parties in the county. 
Kelly Garvin, 3395 Tonto Drive, talked about voter fraud during the election and the
Board being held accountable if any fraud is found. 
Jim Vance, 684 S. Main, Show Low, stated that he is looking forward to the presentation
that will be done, indicating that he hopes to get some answers to the questions that
have been asked.  He talked about how the machines and the steps that they go
through not being a clear and transparent elections, indicating that it is the right of the
people to have same day voting, a hand county and results being posted the same day
or early the next day and that it is the duty of the people to make sure that happens.   
John Major,4834 Oakwood St., Lakeside, stated that the same day voting / in-person
voting will require legislative changes and not just implementations by the Board of
Supervisors.  He indicated that he is concerned with the drop boxes in the county due to
them not being secure, not being monitored and there being no surveillance and that it
needs to be changed.  He stated that the Board has received some petitions requesting
that state law be followed and that they have taken some measures, such as increasing
the precincts, that there should be one polling place per precinct rather than large
precincts with a lot of voting centers, which allows for more supervision and monitoring. 
He encouraged the Board to get rid of the drop boxes or to monitor them.
Steve Carvajal, 650 N. Penrod, stated that he had hoped they would be able to interact
during the presentation, but that he will be next board meeting to be able to voice his
position after the presentation.  He stated as Elected Officials the Board needs to serve
the needs and rights of the citizens in the county.  He indicated that one of the rights is
to have a clean and fair election.  He addressed an issue that occurred in Whiteriver
during the Presidential Preference Election.  He stated that our voting system is in
shambles and indicated that they have a right to address their grievance.
Celia Laughlin, 2169 Hashknife Rd., Overgaard, stated that after viewing evidence from
various new sources across the county, they have lost faith in our election system,
especially with the voting machines.  She indicated that we don’t know who is
programming the machines and that they don’t know the chain of custody once they are
programmed.  She talked about having a backup plan in case the machines go down on
election day and that they prepare for a hand count.  She requested that they be given
thirty days to enroll people to do a hand count and see what the interest is.  She talked
about different movies that address various problems with the machines, such as
electioncode.com.  She requested that the ballots from the 2022 election be kept so that
a forensic audit can be done.
Jerry Hubbard, 2726 Cover Wagon Trail, Overgaard, stated that he has voted in every
election for over 50 years.  He indicated that over the past three years he has been
working to improve election integrity in Navajo County, including becoming a poll worker
for the past few elections.  He talked about being told that the ES&S tabulators are not
connected to the internet and requested that they be able to inspect it for the presence
of a wireless modem installed on the motherboard.  He addressed a report from 2020
regarding ES&S having installed approximately 14,000 modems in DS 200 Tabulators,
allowing them to be connected to the internet.  He requested that they make sure that
                                                                    2 of 12 
DRAFT

<!-- page 10 -->
the software used is voting machines is available for inspection and not tell them that
they can’t be opened due to having been certified by the Elections Assistance
Commission, indicating that they have a lack of credibility.  He stated that if they want a
transparent, free and fair election, there can’t be any secrets.
Donna Sherwin, 1207 S. Forest Lane, Pinetop, offered her services to help find poll
workers.  She talked about the news article in the White Mountain Independent that
talked about how unaffordable it would be to hand count the ballots.  She stated that she
has been a poll worker since 2022 and indicated that if there aren’t advertisements
regarding a need for poll works then nobody will know.  She talked about the incident in
Whiteriver during the Presidential Preference Election.  She addressed the portal for poll
workers on the website not being clear, stating that the transparency is not there and
that thy need to advertise in newspaper.  She talked about doing a hand count and the
amount of money that was spent during elections for poll workers.
James Besua, 3048 S. Pinetop Mountain Lane, Pinetop, stated that all good Americans
should want and demand a fair and honest election, regarding of party affiliation.  He
inquired how they can know that the election results are fair and honest when all they
have is news reports.  He indicated that the voting machines are like all computerized
systems and can be hacked.  He stated that a national survey found that 80% of
Americans believe that the current voting systems can be vulnerable to hackers.  He
talked about the voting machines having modems which makes them vulnerable to
hackers, indicating that once hackers start talking to the voting machines, they can
change the election results or program the machine to cheat on future elections.  He
stated that if Navajo County continues to use the electronic voting machines, they
should be required to remove the modems.  He indicated that the voters depend on the
election representatives to do all they can to ensure a fair, impartial, and honest
system.
Donna Sherwin, 1207 S. Forest Ln., Pinetop, talked about a news article regarding the
overhaul of the General Plan and the community meetings that will be held.  She
inquired when the community meetings will be held, indicating that the article states that
it will be starting in April and going through October.  She stated that the article also
mentions that different organizations will be included in the planning group, which is
referred to as the planning committee.  She further stated that she has not heard
anything about that and that nobody knows about it.  She indicated that she wished that
would be more transparency.  She requested that there be more notifications put out
regarding this and the need for poll workers so that citizens will know about what’s going
on.

c.
Elections Presentation:
Chairman Whiting stated that there have been a lot of questions about
elections and that there were some concerns around it.  He indicated that
they wanted to see what all of the questions and concerns of the citizens
were and then have staff share what the Election process is and
incorporate the questions from citizens into that.  He stated that they
asked the Republican and Democrat party chairs to take the questions that
were received through the website had the party chairs take all of the
questions submitted so that the questions could be consolidated and
incorporated into the presentation.  He indicated that the intent was to
hear the voice of the citizens so that a presentation could be put together
to help everyone understand the processes. He stated that they appreciate
the comments shared today in the Call to the Public.
A presentation was shown.  Bryan Layton stated that there is a lot that
goes into elections which are governed by the State statute and the
Elections Procedures Manuel.  He indicated that there are more details
than can be covered today but that they took the questions asked and that
they will be sharing what they learned.  He stated that they cannot
address what happens in other jurisdictions and can only speak to the
Navajo County processes.  He indicated that they do have a lot of
information that is being provided in the handouts and that it will be placed
on the website, under Elections, for everyone to review.  Mr. Layton talked
about the three main settings for ballots, indicating that there are the vote
centers, where you cast your vote and it’s tallied; then it moves onto
  
                                                                    6 of 12 
DRAFT

<!-- page 14 -->
election central, where the votes are counted; and then it moves onto the
Secretary of State, where it’s aggregated, and then results are provided to
the public.  He reviewed the detailed steps for each process.  He stated
that first they print the ballots, that there are many different ballot styles
depending on where people live and what they vote for, indicating that
during a presidential election that are over 400 different unique ballot
styles. He stated that once they are printed, they are counted, boxed up,
sent to the vote centers where they are inspected, counted, and made
ready for voters.  He indicated that when a voter comes in, they show their
ID, are checked in to the system and once they are checked in and
verified they receive a slip of paper showing that they are a registered
voter, identified for their correct ballot style, provided the ballot where they
can then vote and then they place it in the vote box which is locked up and
secured inside the vote center or if there is a tabulator machine on site
they can tally it on site.  He stated that all of the different votes have one
thing is common that they are all paper driver, that in Navajo County they
do not use any electronic or digital ballots.  He stated that in Navajo
County you can vote at any vote center, that you don’t have to vote within
your precinct.  He stated that with over 400 ballot style, if you go to a
precinct that is far from your home it won’t be likely that they have a
pre-printed ballot for your style, so they would give you a blank ballot,
direct you to an express vote machine, they will put in what ballot style is
needed, then it will print out your results that you will then put in the vote
box or the tabulator machine.  He indicated that the express vote machine
is just a printer, that the paper is what is counted, nothing from the
machine is counted and that it also services the ADA accessibility
requirement in each of the sites.  He stated that the ballots are then
transported by deputies back to the Election Central warehouse, where
they are counted, put together, packaged, and sent to the Secretary of
State.  He stated that the Saturday after the election they are required by
law to do a hand count of the paper ballots.  He indicated that there are a
lot of questions regarding the integrity of that process, and he reviewed the
checks and balances involved, indicating that first there is a chain of
custody that’s followed for every step of the process, second there is
physical security with the tamper resistant seals and seals with barcodes
that feeds into the chain of custody for the ballots and equipment, third the
ballots returned to the elections warehouse must be reconciled so the
number of ballots sent must equal the number of ballots returned including
the unused and spoiled ballots, and forth the number of voters checked in
needs to equal the number of votes that they got back.  He talked about
the second section regarding mail in ballots, indicating that the right to vote
by mail is set by State statute and can’t be changed by the Board of
Supervisors, that it is not an option or legal authority that the Board has.
He reviewed the process, indicating that the ballots are printed, mailed to
those on the Early Active Early Voting list, and that they received the
mailed envelope with a colored envelope, affidavit envelope and the actual
ballot inside.  He stated that once received the individual will complete the
ballot, put it in the verification envelope and sign it, place it in the colored
return envelope.  He stated that it has a unique barcode that identifies the
specific voter and that they can return it by USPS or drop it into an official
ballot drop box.  He indicated that there were several comments regarding
                                                                    7 of 12 
DRAFT

<!-- page 15 -->
having more camera monitoring at the drop boxes, that they do have it for
some but that they have received some grant funding for additional and
that they are currently looking at what equipment would work best to install
on the ballot drop boxes.  He stated that when the ballot is returned the
Recorder scans them to make sure that it is a valid voter, that it is the item
that was sent by Navajo County, that they check and verify the signature,
and once verified the ballot is opened, counted, sent to the Secretary of
State and then a hand count audit is done the Saturday following the
election.  He reviewed the checks and balances for this process,
indicating, first that the ballot/envelope is not forwardable, second for
physical security the drop box is medal and locked, ballots are picked up
by two people one being from each party and a chain of custody, third the
colored envelope has a barcode specific to each voter, forth the ballot
must be signed and the signatures are verified against the voters
signature of file, and fifth the voter is checked in on the system to prevent
the person from voting again.  Mr. Layton addressed the ballot security,
indicating that the Election Central building is highly restricted with only
select Elections, IT, Sheriff’s Office, and Facilities staff having access, that
there is a security system that records entry in and out of the building, that
processes involving ballots and counting verification is done by two people
and that on election nights everything is streamed live.  He talked about
the cybersecurity that they have in place, indicating that they have monthly
meetings with local cybersecurity professionals up to the national level that
help them become aware of emerging threats.  He talked about the
different equipment, including the poll pad which has special software and
is used to check in and verify voters before voting, that it is done in real
time, that each voting center has a hotspot that has a masked Wi-Fi
connection, that the Wi-Fi hotspot is encrypted and then it’s encrypted to a
local file and that it does not go to the Secretary of State.  He stated that
the Secretary of State will only accept information from Navajo County
through a dedicated gateway computer, which is a secure line, and will not
accept information from any other source of transmission.  He talked about
the express voting machines, indicating that it provides ADA accessibility
that is required by law, the onsite tabulators that are counting machines
used to tally the votes, and the central count machines, stating that they
do not have external connections.  He indicated that data isn’t transmitted
over Wi-Fi, that they use two types of flash drives.  He stated that one is
proprietary, that it is unique to some of the equipment, that it has a special
coding on it and will not work on just any computer and that they are used
to load different new ballot styles for each election onto the machines.  He
further stated that they also have regular flash drivers that are scanned
and cleared by IT, that they are brand new and are used to take
information that is aggregated and put it on the elections computer and the
gateway’s computer just to make sure that there are no connections or
transmissions, so they know exactly what is going onto the machine.  He
talked about transmitting the data, indicating that first the results are put all
together on a dedicated machine, that the computer is not on the county
network or any other connection, that it is then put on the gateway
computer that has a secure connection to the Secretary
 of State,
that it has never been connected to the internet and cant connect to
anything besides the Secretary of State secure portal, which is extremely
                                                                    8 of 12 
DRAFT

<!-- page 16 -->
firewalled and that only one person has access to log into that computer.
He stated that once everything has been sent a report from the Secretary
of State is downloaded to verify what they received.  He reviewed the
checks and balances for the machines, indicating that first a logic and
accuracy test is done pre-election and that the political parties not only
witness but help with the test and that it must pass with one hundred
percent accuracy, second is the physical security where they box and seal
the equipment, third is the hand count audit that is done the Saturday after
the election where they take votes from one random vote center that had
an on-site tabulator, they run them through and count them and it must
pass with one hundred percent accuracy and then forth they do a
post-election logic and accuracy test where they have multiple batches of
different types of ballots that are run through and must pass with a
hundred percent accuracy every time.  He talked about the Voter
Registration Lists, indicating that there are a lot of federal and state
restrictions on how people can be removed and how it’s maintained.  He
stated that the three main ways to be removed are first when they receive
a download from that State for a death, second, they received information
from the courts if you’re a convicted felon or found mentally incapacitated,
and third an address change which is received from the post office or the
voter, and that they also have a system that operates in thirty-one other
states and the District of Columbia that helps them.  He stated that it’s a
manual process, that they receive reports and have to make the changes
manually.  He talked about the questions related to the number of
registered voters exceeding the number of voting age adults recorded in
the census, indicating that there are a combination of factors that come
into play, such as the census taking place during COVID, which was very
difficult, and they believe that Navajo County was under counted, there
being a large number of part-time residents or snowbirds that are seasonal
homeowners that may be registered to vote in Navajo County but may
have been counted elsewhere in the census and there being seventeen
year olds on the voting list, which is legal and in the state statute that if you
are seventeen turning eighteen prior to the next election you can register
to vote, which reflects Navajo County being proactive in getting out to the
schools to educate the seniors on the importance of voting and being
involved in the communities.  He talked about the process for being able to
remove inactive voters that have not voted in two general election cycles. 
He spoke about the hand count, indicating that Mohave County did look
into it last year and that some of their takeaways were, first that there
would need to be a lot more people involved with could lead to
confidentiality risk and that confidentiality is required by law, second,
timing due to the strict timelines it would require a lot of additional staff to
be hired to support the effort, third, was accuracy indicating that there was
an error rate of 5.4% with the 850 ballots and forth, was a concern with
logistics and cost with finding somewhere big enough to accommodate the
number of people it would take and the amount that would be spent
indicating that Mohave County estimated it would cost as much as $1.1
million to do a full hand count.  He stated that currently we don’t
experience concerns with confidentiality, or meeting state deadlines, that
we have witnessed and audited one hundred percent accuracy and that
we don’t have one million dollars in discretionary revenue available in the
                                                                    9 of 12 
DRAFT

<!-- page 17 -->
budget.  He talked about looking into how Navajo County can better
advertise the need for help during the elections.  He talked about where
the authorities and jurisdictions are for a lot of the questions asked,
indicating that most of the authority comes from the state.  He summarized
all of the information that was provided, indicating that Navajo County only
uses paper ballots, ballots are hand counted to audit results after the
elections, that there is on-demand verification of mail-in ballots and at vote
centers to prevent voting twice, that checks and balances exist to identify
attempts to stuff the ballot boxes or the drop boxes, that the Secretary of
State will only accept vote results from Navajo County from our dedicated
and secured line portal computer, that the right to vote by mail is set by
statute and can’t be changed by the Board, that voter registration lists are
a constant work in progress that is manual and tough with all of the
regulations, that for a hand count the experience of others shows that it
would take longer, introduce errors and have higher costs, that the Board
has the responsibility to carry out elections but has very little discretion or
authority granted by statute to alter how elections are covered, that in
Navajo County there are check, balances and securities in place so that
ballots are counted and reported correctly, and that we have great people
who are professionals and trying their best to conduct elections with
integrity and support our communities. 
Supervisor Seymore stated that he appreciates the time that staff has
taken on this to work through the processes.  He talked about some of the
Legislative people that attend their CSA meeting and talked about election
integrity.  He thanked the citizens that provided input today.  He talked
about working with the citizens to have transparency, indicating that they
are here to make it fair and honest the best they can but that they are also
regulated.  He talked about voter integrity and everyone and doing their
part.  He challenged those present to encourage people to vote and not
that their vote doesn’t count, indicating that in Navajo County everyone’s
vote is being counted.     
Supervisor Whitesinger shared her appreciation to those that expressed
their concerns, indicating that when the public comes and participates it is
important.  She stated that it opens an avenue for conservations and being
able to answer the questions.  She shared her gratitude to Mr. Layton and
staff for the presentation and for answering the questions of the public. 
She talked about the importance of protecting the rights of voters.  She
shared her appreciation to all those who assist with elections to make sure
that every vote is counted. 
Vice-Chairwoman Benally thanked Mr. Layton for the presentation.  She
spoke about the constituents on the Navajo Nation having problems voting
due to transportation, weather and road issues and indicated that the mail
in ballots and drop boxes are important for them being able to vote, that
they don’t want them to be disenfranchised and that they want all of them
to have an opportunity to vote when it comes to elections.
Supervisor Peshlakai thanked Mr. Layton and staff for putting together
the presentation.  He stated that he has been voting since he was
eighteen and talked about voting by paper ballot until transitioning to the
express machine.  He spoke about the details presented regarding mail-in
ballots and being able to vote in any vote center in Navajo County,
indicating that it is a positive thing, especially for the Navajo Nation so that
                                                                    10 of 12 
DRAFT

<!-- page 18 -->
people can go to a vote center rather than having to report back to their
Chapters.  He stated that seeing the process from start to finish has been
an eye opener knowing how much security there is and that he has faith in
our elections team as well as the poll workers.  He talked about his
experience when voting during the Presidential Preference Election.  He
talked about the security measures with Navajo County Elections
compared to those on the Navajo Nation. 
Chairman Whiting inquired how all is present for the pre-election
equipment audit?
Mr. Layton stated that it is Election staff, a representative from the
Democrat and Republican parties, Law Enforcement, and individuals from
the State.
Chairman Whiting inquired if the equipment is opened up for them to look
inside during the audit?
Mr. Layton stated that he does not believe it is opened. He stated that they
create a sample ballot deck and run it through in different combinations
approximately ten different times to make sure they are getting consistent
results.
Chairman Whiting inquired what happens with it after the audit if it is
sealed?
Mr. Layton stated that it is sealed, that they have the physical production of
the elections building, that within there the equipment is boxed, sealed to
identify tampering, and then it’s behind lock and key until election day.   
Chairman Whiting inquired who all is present for the post-election audit?
Mr. Layton stated that the Saturday after the election they do the hand
count and that they are required to have the same individuals present,
representatives from the Democrat and Republican parties, Elections staff
and Law Enforcement. 
Chairman Whiting thanked Mr. Layton for the information.  He spoke
about the information shared related to Mohave County, indicating that
there was some good information shared on that relating to the cost
prospective and the confidentiality.  He requested that Mr. Layton review
the information provided regarding there being no concern with Navajo
County not being connected to the internet.
Mr. Layton talked about the Secretary of State only receiving vote data
from the county through a specific source stating that the Secretary of
State is completely firewalled and will only accept information from one
source from Navajo County.  He stated that they have the seals and that
the connections are either through a USB or a hardline connection, that
there is no Wi-Fi, Bluetooth, or electronic transmission. 
Chairman Whiting inquired as to the authority for mail-in ballots being
controlled by the State and that it is not something that the Board can
change. 
Mr. Layton stated that the Board of Supervisors has no authority to alter
whether or not they can do mail-in ballots, that it is controlled by the State,
indicating that it is one way to provide ADA accessibility to certain voters
and that it’s in state statute.

Board of Supervisors Regular
2. g.        
Meeting Date:
05/14/2024
 
Title:
High Intensity Drug Trafficking Area MCAT Grant
Submitted For: David Clouse, Sheriff 
Submitted By:
Kimberley Willis, Sheriff's Office Finance Manager
Department:
Sheriff 
Motion before the Board:
High Intensity Drug Trafficking Area grant agreement, HT-24-2993A, in the amount of $126,575.00
Background:
The Sheriff's Office has once again received funding from the High Intensity Drug Trafficking Area
(HIDTA) program through the City of Tucson.  The $126,575.00 award will pay for full salary and
partial (15%) of employee related expenses for our HIDTA Major Crimes Apprehension Team (MCAT)
Lieutenant, in addition to overtime for MCAT detectives, and services/supplies for MCAT's operational
needs.
The grant period begins on January 1, 2024, and terminates December 31, 2025.  Expenditures are
reimbursed upon receipt of financial reports.

Board of Supervisors Regular
2. h.        
Meeting Date:
05/14/2024
 
Title:
Modification of USFS Grant Agreement 22-LE-11030100-014
Submitted For: David Clouse, Sheriff 
Submitted By:
Kimberley Willis, Sheriff's Office Finance Manager
Department:
Sheriff 
Motion before the Board:
Modification Number 002 to grant/agreement 22-LE-11030100-014 with the USDA Forest Service
adding $10,000 in funding and updating the USFS Program Manager to Matthew N. Ryan
Background:
The Sheriff's Office has entered into a cooperative agreement with the US Forest Service for law
enforcement efforts within the Apache-Sitgreaves National Forests.  This modification (002) to
agreement 22-LE-11030100-014 adds $10,000 for calendar year 2024 and updates the administrative
contact at USFS. Pertaining to this agreement, funds are received quarterly upon receipt of financial,
narrative reports, and patrol logs.  The performance period of this grant/agreement is January 1, 2022 -
December 31, 2026.  The cooperative agreement reimburses Navajo County for patrols occurring on
select USFS roads, campgrounds, developed sites, and dispersed areas.  Wages will be reimbursed at the
average prevailing rate not to exceed $45.00 an hour.  Mileage will be reimbursed at the average
prevailing rate not to exceed $0.81 per mile.

Motion before the Board:

Background:
On March 14, 2023, the Board approved Contract No. B23-01-003 with Advent Financial Systems, LLC.
for the Juvenile Diversion Program.  This contract has worked well for Navajo County. The Juvenile
Diversion Program Contract may be renewed up to four additional one-year terms. This would be the first
renewal.  Both parties are in concurrence. Staff recommends that the contract be extended for one
additional year from March 14, 2024, through March 13, 2025.

2. j.

Title:
State Consent Agreement
Submitted For: Ken Dewitt, Information Technology Director 
Submitted By:
Ken Dewitt, Information Technology Director
Department:
Information Technology
Motion before the Board:
State and Local Grant Consent Agreement
Background:
This is a continuation of the State and Local Cybersecurity program that allows local government
agencies to install and currently use 5 different programs that protect our network.  We have been using
this for the past 4 years.  It saves the County over $200,000.00 each year. 
The 5 programs provide visibility in these areas.
Anti-Phishing / Security Awareness Training (SAT)
Advanced Endpoint Protection (AEP)
Converged Endpoint Management (XEM)
Multi-Factor Authentication (MFA)
Web Application Firewall (WAF)

part of the Federal Fiscal Year (FFY) 2023 SLCGP, and instead prefers to receive pass-through funding.

the following acts in accordance with the State and Local Cybersecurity Grant Program (SLCGP) for
Federal Fiscal Year (FFY) 2023, Funding Opportunity Number DHS-23-GPD-137-00-01, as authorized by
Section 2220A of the Homeland Security Act of 2002, as amended (Pub. L. No. 107-296) (6 U.S.C. § 665g):
a.
Retaining and passing through to local government entities at least 80% of the State of
Arizona's FFY 2023 SLCGP grant award of $6,776,692 in the form of services and related
activities; and
b.

(1) $1,585,075.00 for Project 1: Cybersecurity Software Licenses
(2) $2,500,000.00 for Project 2: 24x7x365 Centralized State Security Operations Center
(3) $2,352,782.40 for Project 3: Professional/Managed Services to support the Statewide Cyber
Readiness Program

funds from the SLCGP. I also agree that this Local Consent Agreement is only effective for the Federal Fiscal Year
(FFY) 2023 SLCGP Funds, and this Local Consent Agreement and receipt of funds, under either selection above, are
contingent upon submission and approval of a project that aligns with the State’s approved Cybersecurity Plan.

Eric Molnar, Kristie Blackman, Deborah Myers, Jeff Pearce, Walter Blackman, Anne Ascoli, Roger
Gorres, Mark McKinlay, Earl Strecker, Gary Olmstead, Bethany Olmstead-Wittren, Steven Wittren,
Christine Etchison, Rod Campbell, Angela Campbell, Wyatt Buschschulte, Kay Christensen, Mary Faltz,
Nancy Telizyn, Anthony Tangalos as Republican Precinct Committeemen for their respective precincts
Background:
Appointment valid through Oct. 1, 2026.
16-822. Precinct committeemen; eligibility; vacancy; duties; term B.  If the number of persons who file
nominating petitions for an election to fill precinct committeeman positions is less than or equal to the
number of precinct committeeman positions, the county board of supervisors may cancel the election for
those positions not sooner than seventy-five days before the election and appoint the person who filed the
nominating petition to fill the position. If no person has filed a nominating petition to fill a position, the
position is deemed vacant and shall be filled as otherwise provided by law. A precinct committeeman
who is appointed pursuant to this subsection after filing a nominating petition shall be deemed an elected
precinct committeeman. F. The term of office of a precinct committeeman is two years and begins on
October 1 after the primary election at which the precinct committeeman was a candidate and continues
until October 1 after the following primary election at which a precinct committeeman is elected.

Foy, Steven Gorman-Hackstadt, Janis Newton, Dorothy Reed-Inman, Eric Kramer, Linda Hoy, James
Burress, Ramona Patterson, Christina Burress, David Alexander, Marianne Richardson, Martha Kafer
LaMar, Casey Lee, Larry Romo and Rosezene Vetaw as Democrat Precinct Committeemen for their
respective precincts
Background:
Appointment valid through Oct. 1, 2026.
16-822. Precinct committeemen; eligibility; vacancy; duties; term B.  If the number of persons who file
nominating petitions for an election to fill precinct committeeman positions is less than or equal to the
number of precinct committeeman positions, the county board of supervisors may cancel the election for
those positions not sooner than seventy-five days before the election and appoint the person who filed the
nominating petition to fill the position. If no person has filed a nominating petition to fill a position, the
position is deemed vacant and shall be filled as otherwise provided by law. A precinct committeeman
who is appointed pursuant to this subsection after filing a nominating petition shall be deemed an elected
precinct committeeman. F. The term of office of a precinct committeeman is two years and begins on
October 1 after the primary election at which the precinct committeeman was a candidate and continues
until October 1 after the following primary election at which a precinct committeeman is elected.

CONTRACT FOR PROFESSIONAL SERVICES 
Agreement made as of May 1, 2024 between Navajo County ("County") and Tony Willis 
("Consultant") with an effective date to begin June 1, 2024. 
Whereas, County desires to utilize professional coaching services for staff development and 
training and; 
Whereas, Consultant has proper qualifications and experience to provide such services and; 
Whereas, the County recognizes that the consultant is qualified to provide said services to 
provide the desired outcome of County staff for both development and training.  
Now, Therefore, the parties agree as follows: 
1. 
Services. Consultant shall provide services in a competent and professional manner as 
outlined in attached document titled Attachment A, to the satisfaction of the County. 
2. 
Consultant's Expertise. Consultant warrants that it has the ability, authority, capacity and 
professional expertise to perform these services. Consultant shall self-perform this 
agreement and be devoted to the successful accomplishment of the work to be performed 
hereunder. 
3. 
Independent Contractor. Consultant acknowledges that it is an independent contractor. 
Neither party is or shall be an agent, employee, partner, joint venture or associate of the 
other. An employee or agent of one party shall not be deemed an employer or agent of the 
other party for any purpose. 
4. 
Subcontracts. Consultant shall not enter into any subcontract with respect to any of the 
work to be performed hereunder without the County's prior written approval. All 
subcontracts shall comply with applicable federal and state laws and regulations and shall 
impose on the subcontractor substantially the same obligations as are imposed on 
Consultant by this Agreement with respect to those matters covered herein. Consultant is 
responsible for full performance of this Agreement regardless of whether subcontractors are 
used or not. 
5. 
Term of Contract. Consultant's services shall be performed on an on-going basis. It is 
anticipated that the need for services will continue for multiple years and the initial term 
of the contract shall be for 12 months and may be renewed upon mutual agreement by 
both parties. 
6. 
Payment. County shall pay Consultant a monthly rate as outlined in Attachment A.  
Payments shall be due and payable upon receipt of Consultant's itemized invoice. Each 
invoice shall detail the work performed during the billing period.

<!-- page 163 -->
7. 
Defects in Work. County may reject any work product that fails to meet customary 
professional standards or Project specifications. Consultant agrees to promptly remedy all 
such deficiencies. The parties shall make a good faith effort to resolve any controversy or 
claim through informal negotiation as set forth in Section 13. No compensation shall be 
paid for any rejected work until such issues have been resolved. 
8. 
Ownership of Work Product. Consultant understands that all work product developed by 
him alone or in conjunction with others in connection with the performance of services 
pursuant to this Agreement is and shall be the sole property of the County, and Consultant 
shall retain no ownership, interest, or rights therein. Work product includes but is not limited 
to analysis, reports, graphics, memoranda, slogans, and taglines. 
9. 
Indemnity. To the fullest extent allowable by law, Consultant shall defend, indemnify and 
hold harmless County and its representatives , officers, officials and employees (the 
"indemnitees") from and against all claims, damages, losses and expenses (including but 
not limited to attorney’s fees, court costs, and the costs of appellate proceedings) relating 
to, arising out of or resulting from Consultant's negligent acts, errors, mistakes or omissions 
in the performance of this Agreement. Consultant's duty to defend, hold harmless and 
indemnify the indemnitees shall arise in connection with any claim, damage, loss or 
expense that is attributable to bodily injury, sickness, disease, death or injury to, impairment 
or destruction of property including loss of use resulting therefrom cause whole or in part by 
any negligent act, error, mistake or omission in the performance of this Agreement 
(including those by any person for whose negligent acts, errors, mistakes or omissions 
Consultant may be liable) to the extent of Consultant's negligence or fault. 
10. 
Records. Consultant shall retain and shall require each subcontractor (if applicable) to 
retain, all books, accounts, reports, files and other records relating to this Agreement for a 
period of five years after completion of the work. All such documents shall be subject to 
inspection and audit by County upon reasonable notice during normal business hours. A 
legible copy of any or all such documents shall be produced by Consultant at the request 
and expense of County. 
11. 
Confidentiality: 
a. 
Definition-"CONFIDENTIAL INFORMATION" as used in this Agreement shall mean 
any and all technical and non-technical information including patent, copyright, trade 
secret, proprietary information, computer files, and County information related to the 
past, current, future, and proposed services of the County and includes, without 
limitation, County property, and County information concerning research, financial 
information, purchasing, forecasts, and plans, policies and objectives. 
b. 
Nondisclosure and Nonuse Obligations 
Consultant agrees to protect the confidentiality of all Confidential Information and, 
except as permitted in this section, Consultant shall neither use nor disclose the 
Confidential Information. Consultant may use the Confidential Information solely 
to perform consulting services under this Agreement for the benefit of the County. 
 
c. 
Exclusion from Nondisclosure and Nonuse Obligations 
Consultant's obligations under NONDISCLOSURE AND NONUSE OBLIGATIONS 
with respect to any portion of the Confidential Information shall not apply to any

<!-- page 164 -->
such portion that Consultant can demonstrate (a) was in the public domain at or 
subsequent to the time such portion was communicated to Consultant by the 
County; (b) was rightfully in Consultant's possession free of any obligation of 
confidence at or subsequent to the time such portion was communicated to 
Consultant by the County; or (c) was developed by Consultant independently of and 
without reference to any information communicated to Consultant by the County. A 
disclosure of Confidential Information by Consultant, either (i) in response to a valid 
order by a court or other governmental body, (ii) otherwise required by law, or (iii) 
necessary to establish the rights of either party under this Agreement, shall not be 
considered a breach of this Agreement or a waiver of confidentiality for other 
purposes, provided, however , that Consultant shall provide prompt written notice 
thereof to the County to enable the County to seek a protective order or 
otherwise prevent such disclosure . 
 
12. 
Non-Assignment. Consultant shall not assign any right in this Agreement without County's 
prior written approval, nor shall Consultant delegate any duty hereunder without County's 
prior written approval. 
13. 
Negotiation of Disputes. The parties shall make a good faith effort to resolve any claim or 
controversy or claim through informal negotiation. Notice of any claim or controversy shall 
be provided in writing, with supporting documentation, to the recipient designated in Section 
23. The recipient shall have seven calendar days to prepare and deliver a written response. 
If the parties fail to resolve the disputes within a reasonable period of not less than ten days, 
either party may pursue available legal remedies. 
14. 
Suspension and Termination by County: 
a. 
Suspension. County may, without cause, order Consultant in writing to suspend, 
delay or interrupt its performance in whole or part. An adjustment shall be made to 
the completion date and for any increase in cost of performance resulting from the 
suspension, delay or interruption. No adjustment shall be made to the extent that 
performance is or would have been suspended, delayed or interrupted by another 
cause for which Consultant is responsible. 
b. 
Termination for Cause. County may terminate this Agreement for cause if 
Consultant refuses or fails to perform this Agreement; disregards laws, ordinances, 
rules, regulations or orders of a public authority having jurisdiction; or otherwise 
commits a material breach of this Agreement. When any of the foregoing causes 
exist, County shall give Consultant written notice and a ten-day opportunity to cure 
the default. If the default remains uncured, County may (without prejudice to any 
rights or remedies it may have) terminate this agreement and finish the work or 
cause it to be finished. If the unpaid balance of the Agreement exceeds the cost of 
finishing the work (including expenses incurred by the County as a result of the 
Consultant's default), Consultant shall be entitled to payment for its performance up 
to the amount of such excess. If the cost of finishing the work exceeds the unpaid 
balance, Consultant shall pay the difference to County. 
c. 
Termination for Convenience. County, by 30 day advance written notice to 
Consultant, may terminate this agreement in whole or part when in the sole 
discretion of County, it is in County's best interests to do so. Consultant shall be 
paid for all services provided.

<!-- page 165 -->
d. 
Consultant's Responsibility. Upon receipt of a termination notice, Consultant shall 
(a) promptly discontinue all services (unless notice directs otherwise), and (b) deliver 
or otherwise make available to County copies of all data, reports, summaries and 
other information and materials developed or accumulated by Consultant in 
performing this Agreement. 
15. 
Termination by Consultant. Consultant may terminate this agreement upon 30 day 
written notice to County. Consultant may also terminate by written notice if performance is 
made impossible for a period of thirty (30) consecutive calendar days for any of the 
following reasons through no act or fault of Consultant or its agents, employees or 
subcontractors: issuance of an order by a count or other public authority having jurisdiction; 
an act of government, such as a declaration of national emergency; or a natural disaster or 
other Act of God. Consultant may also terminate this agreement upon written notice and a 
ten-day opportunity to cure if County fails to make any payment set forth in Section 6. 
Consultant may also terminate this agreement upon written notice if suspensions, delays or 
interruptions by County equal in the aggregate more than 100% of the total number of days 
scheduled for completion. 
16. 
Governing Law. This agreement shall be governed by the law of the State of Arizona. 
Any suit arising out of this agreement shall be brought in the state courts of Arizona (with 
venue in Navajo County) or federal District of Arizona, but only after informal negotiation 
pursuant to Section 13. 
 
17. 
Incorporation of Applicable Laws. Every provision of law required by statute or 
regulation to be in this Agreement will be read and enforced as though included herein. 
Each party shall promptly notify the other upon discovery that any such provision has been 
omitted. 
18. 
Non-Collusion. The provisions A.R.S . 38-511 are incorporated herein by this reference. 
19. 
One-Year Limitation on Actions. No action shall be maintained by Consultant on any 
claim based upon or arising out of this agreement unless such action is commenced within 
one year after the County's final payment hereunder. 
20. 
Term. This Contract for Professional Service shall begin on June 1, 2024 and is valid for a 
twelve (12) month period from that date. Upon mutual agreement between the parties, this 
contract may be automatically renewed for up to two (2) additional twelve (12) month 
terms. 
21. 
Entire Agreement. This agreement and those documents incorporated by reference 
represent the entire understanding between the parties. No amendment shall be effective 
unless executed by both parties with the same formality as this agreement. Provided, 
however, that the County's designated representative is authorized to modify the Scope of 
Work, in writing, with the concurrence of Consultant so long as the project is not changed 
substantially and/or significant additional compensation is not required. 
22. 
Severability. The provisions of this agreement are severable. Any provision held to be 
invalid or unenforceable shall not affect the validity or enforceability of any other provision.

<!-- page 167 -->
ATTACHMENT A 
 
Qualifications and Bio: Tony Willis 
 
Tony began his career in the airline industry at Phoenix based America West Airlines. During his 20-year 
career at America West and US Airways, Tony developed the ability to interact effectively with all levels of 
employees, from front line to executives. He became skilled at building strong team environments, 
fostering open communication, and improving employee engagement. As Director of Leadership 
Development, he had the opportunity to coach employees and leaders at all levels of the organization. 
 
In 2008 a career change took Tony and his family to Houston, Texas. He worked in the oil and gas 
industry for a couple of offshore drilling companies as Director of Leadership Development. He was able 
to coach leaders from front line managers to C-Suite level. He also conducted leadership development 
classes in various locations around the world. Even though traveling to interesting locations was fun and 
exciting, the best part for Tony was meeting great people all around the world and learning about different 
cultures.  
 
As an Arizona native (born and raised in Snowflake, Arizona) Tony was glad when he could finally return 
home to the Grand Canyon State in 2019. After a short year working for Freedom Financial Network 
developing their leadership team, he found his way to Sunstate Equipment, as the Leadership 
Development Instructor. 
 
Since retiring from full time corporate work in 2022, he enjoys hiking and camping in the beautiful Arizona 
desert. He also loves to spend time with his wife, kids and grandkids. 
 
Leader Coaching Expectations 
 
For the purposes of this proposal, “Coach” refers to the contracted consultant, “Coachee” refers to the 
leader being coached, and “Internal Sponsor” refers to the coachee’s supervisor or manager. 
1. Enrolling the Coachee and Building the Relationship: The coaching process is effective and 
successful for clients when a trusting partnership is created and nurtured between the coach and 
the coachee at the onset. The initial coach-coachee session is intended to gather important and 
foundational information, to further orient the coachee to what coaching can achieve, and to enlist 
the commitment of the coachee and his/her internal sponsor(s) to the process. (Billed at the hourly 
coaching rate.) 
2. Fact Finding Assessments and Feedback: It is critical to clarify and understand the 
organization’s perspective and the coachee’s present situation. During this phase, the leadership 
coach will gather specific information about the coachee’s role and current challenges that are 
relevant to each leader’s situation. The assessment instruments recommended include: 
• 
The DiSC Assessment (one time cost of $50) 
• 
Strengths Finder 2.0 Assessment (one time cost of $60) 
• 
360 Feedback (one time cost of $100) 
3. Development Planning: Following assessment and feedback, the leader will create with their 
coach a personalized, written development plan. The plan will provide clear steps and milestones 
for the leader to reach his/her chosen goals. (Billed at the hourly coaching rate.) 
4. Sponsor Meeting: It is recommended that a meeting be held between the coach, the coachee, 
and their internal sponsor. This meeting serves to align the coachee’s development plan with their 
sponsoring manager’s perspective and emerging business needs. The coach facilitates the 
discussion where the direction and goals are agreed upon. This meeting can also be conducted 
again at the end of the coaching engagement to review progress made and goals completed. 
(Billed at the hourly coaching rate.)

<!-- page 168 -->
5. Coaching to the Development Plan: This phase of work involves coaching to the plan and, 
importantly, to real-time issues. The coach will help the coachee become aware of strengths to 
leverage, issues recognized by others as areas to improve upon, and specific actions that are 
timely and achievable. The coach and coachee will establish regular meeting times (two 90-
minute meetings per month is recommended), either face to face or by video call, to discuss the 
ongoing implementation of the development plan. (Billed at the hourly coaching rate.) 
6. “Check-in” Meetings with the Internal Sponsor: The coachee’s sponsor, the HR partner (as 
appropriate), the coachee and his/her coach will meet periodically to ensure that everyone is up to 
date on the progress. These meetings serve to align accountabilities and commitments for support 
and follow through. These meetings will be approximately 30 minutes in length and be held once a 
month (or as needed) either in person or by video call. (Billed at the hourly coaching rate.) 
7. Final Feedback & Planning Session: This final meeting brings the Leader Coaching 
engagement to closure and outlines the plan for ongoing development after the coaching 
engagement. (Billed at the hourly coaching rate.)

Title:
Agreement with Educational Services LLC (ESI) for Professional Staffing Services 
Submitted For: Eric Scott, HR Director 
Submitted By: Eric Scott, HR Director
Department:
Administrative Services
Division:
Human Resources
Motion before the Board:
Agreement with ESI for Professional Staffing Services 
Background:
Educational Services, LLC, (ESI) provides staffing services for Arizona State Retirement System (ASRS)
retirees. Under the ASRS 20/20 rule, a retiree has certain return to work restrictions, that an employee of
ESI would not. This agreement would allow Navajo County to hire staff from ESI, that may have just
retired from Navajo County, at a full-time capacity. Navajo County staff has reached out to ASRS directly
and confirmed that this approach is compliant with their rules.

ESI2024 – GOVT 
-1- 
 
 
 
Employee Staffing Agreement 
 
This Agreement for Employee Staffing Services (“Agreement”) is entered into in 
the State of Arizona effective July 1, 2024 (“Effective Date”), by and between  
Navajo County (“Client”), and Educational Services, LLC, an Arizona limited liability 
company (“ESI”). 
 
RECITALS 
 
A. 
ESI is a company in the business of providing employee staffing services. 
 
B. 
Client is an entity within the State of Arizona and desires to obtain certain staffing 
services (“Services”) from ESI. ESI is willing to provide Services to Client upon the 
terms and conditions contained in this Agreement. 
 
C. 
This Agreement provides for the allocation between Client and ESI of responsibilities 
with respect to covered employees (“Workers” pursuant to Section 2 below). 
 
D. 
This Agreement shall not diminish, abolish, or remove any rights of Workers against 
the Client, or obligations of the Client to any Workers, if any, that existed before the 
Effective Date of this Agreement. 
 
E. 
Client and ESI agree to be bound by the terms and conditions set forth in any 
applicable cooperative contract purchasing agreement. 
 
 
AGREEMENT 
 
In consideration of the foregoing recitals, and mutual promises contained herein, Client 
and ESI agree as follows: 
 
1. 
RELATIONSHIP OF THE PARTIES 
 
 
ESI is an independent contractor and shall perform its obligations under this 
Agreement as an independent contractor. All Workers provided by ESI to perform 
Services for Client pursuant to this Agreement shall be employees of ESI and not of 
Client. Client has the right to direct Workers only to the extent necessary to conduct 
the Client’s business, and operations, and to comply with licensing and certification 
requirements that apply to the Client, or to any Worker. In all other respects, ESI 
retains full control over the employment, direction, supervision, evaluation, 
compensation, discipline, and discharge of Workers performing Services under this 
Agreement. Nothing contained in this Agreement will be construed to create a joint 
venture or partnership, or the relationship of principal and agent, or employer and 
employee, between ESI and Client.

<!-- page 172 -->
ESI2024 – GOVT 
-2- 
 
 
2. 
WORKER 
 
 
In this Agreement, the term “Worker” or “Workers” means an individual(s): (a) 
employed by ESI in Arizona to work in Arizona, (b) who is performing Services for 
Client pursuant to this Agreement, (c) who has completed ESI’s required hiring and 
onboarding process forms, and, where applicable, is certificated or licensed as 
required by law for the position in which ESI places the Worker.  
 
 
ESI will not place into employment positions with the Client any Worker who does 
not possess, or have the ability to possess, all necessary certification and 
endorsements or licenses for the position assigned. 
 
 
ESI will maintain equal employment opportunity and anti-discrimination policies, 
including complaint procedures that address discrimination, and/or harassment 
claims.  ESI retains the right to make a final determination as to whether to hire a 
Worker.  
 
3. 
TERM OF AGREEMENT 
 
 
The initial term of this Agreement will be one (1) year following the Effective Date 
(“Term”). Upon the expiration of the initial Term, this Agreement may be renewed 
annually upon mutual agreement of both parties (with each one-year renewal being a 
successive “Term”). This Agreement does not automatically renew at the end of each 
year. 
 
4. 
NON-APPROPRIATION CLAUSE 
 
 
Client may cancel this Agreement by providing written notice to ESI if funding is not 
available to Client due to budget constraints. 
 
5. 
TERMINATION 
 
A. 
Workers, Without Cause. Notwithstanding any other provision of this 
Agreement, Client may request termination of, and ESI may terminate, any 
Worker at any time without cause upon the submission of at least thirty (30) 
calendar days’ advance written notice. 
 
B. 
Workers, With Cause.  Notwithstanding any other provision of this Agreement, 
Client may request termination of, and ESI may terminate, any Worker upon 
written notice to ESI upon the occurrence of any of the following: 
 
(1) A material breach by ESI, or a Worker of any of ESI’s or Worker’s 
obligations under this Agreement, or under the Worker’s contract and/or 
employment offer letter.

<!-- page 173 -->
ESI2024 – GOVT 
-3- 
 
(2) If a Worker embezzles or misappropriates Client funds or property, 
defrauds Client, is convicted of a felony, or of any crime involving moral 
turpitude, has his or her certification or other licensing required for the 
position for which employed by ESI revoked or suspended, fails to 
maintain a valid fingerprint card if one is required by Client, commits an 
act or omission which constitutes a breach of the Worker’s contract and/or 
employment offer letter, violates the policies of the Client applicable to 
Client’s own employees, commits an act of unprofessional conduct,  or 
commits an act that adversely affects the reputation of Client. 
 
(3) Death or Permanent Disability of a Worker occurring any time during the 
term of this Agreement, in which event this Agreement (as it relates to the 
Worker) shall terminate as of his or her death or Permanent Disability. 
“Permanent Disability” shall mean the Worker is unable to perform his/her 
essential job duties, with or without reasonable accommodation, for a 
period of more than sixty (60) days. 
 
(4) If it is later discovered that a Worker has made any material 
misrepresentations or has failed to provide any material representations in 
connection with the information provided to ESI. 
 
C. 
Agreement.  This Agreement may be terminated by either party, for any reason, 
at any time prior to the expiration of the Term, by providing ninety-day (90) 
written notice to the other Party, in the manner described in Section 28.  
 
6. 
SCOPE OF SERVICES 
 
 
In collaboration with Client, ESI shall supply Workers and shall perform the 
following services: 
 
A. 
Recruit, hire, train, evaluate, compensate, place, replace, supervise, discipline, 
and terminate Workers. 
  
B. 
Maintain a recruiting and hiring program that is in compliance with federal and 
state laws, rules and regulations, equal opportunity, and anti-discrimination 
policies applicable to, and restricting, the hiring and selection process, 
including, but not limited to, Title VII of the Civil Rights Act of 1964 (“Title 
VII”), the Americans With Disabilities Act (“ADA”), the Age Discrimination 
in Employment Act (“ADEA”), the Fair Credit Reporting Act (“FCRA”), the 
Arizona Civil Rights Act (“ACRA”), and the Arizona Employment Protection 
Act (“AEPA”). 
 
C. 
Maintain a system of statewide background checks on all Workers provided to 
Client to include pre-screening, credentialing, licensure, statewide criminal 
background check, and fingerprinting, the results of which shall be made 
available to Client upon request to the extent permitted by law. ESI shall ensure

that all Workers possess all certifications and licenses necessary to perform 
their assignments. 
 
D. 
Maintain a system of evaluation, which can be the Client’s evaluation systems 
and instruments. 
 
E. 
Maintain a program of supervision that enforces appropriate policies and 
procedures. In order to maintain the program, ESI may designate one or more 
on-site ESI employees as the supervisor, and/or, ESI contact responsible for 
addressing and responding to Workers.  
 
F. 
Provide each Worker with information regarding his or her obligation to comply 
with applicable safety, drug/alcohol, anti-harassment, anti-discrimination, anti-
retaliation, and conduct policies.  
 
G. 
Inform each Worker in writing that s/he is employed by ESI, and not employed 
by the Client. 
 
H. 
Inform each Worker in writing that job related illness/injury reports are to be 
made to the Client, and ESI’s on-site supervisor, or ESI contact, and provide 
information on where and how reports are to be made to the Client and ESI 
supervisor or contact. Worker must coordinate with ESI, or ESI’s 
representative, for non-emergency treatment, and not the Client, or Client’s 
representative. 
 
I. 
Pay Workers in compliance with applicable wage and hour laws, including, but 
not limited to the Fair Labor Standards Act (“FLSA”), the Fair Wages and 
Healthy Families Act, and Arizona Labor Code. ESI shall maintain complete 
and accurate records of all wages paid to a Worker assigned to provide services 
to Client. ESI shall be exclusively responsible for, and will comply with, 
applicable law governing the reporting and payment of wages, payroll-related, 
and unemployment taxes attributable to wages paid to Workers assigned to 
provide services to Client.  
 
For SubSource Workers (substitutes), ESI will track, account and pay for paid 
sick time in accordance with the Fair Wages and Healthy Families Act 
(“FWHFA”). These costs for SubSource Workers will not be invoiced to the 
Client and will be ESI’s responsibility. 
 
For RetireRehire Workers (return-to-work retirees), Client will be responsible 
for providing paid leave equal to or greater than the amount required under the 
FWHFA. The annual allotment of a RetireRehire Worker’s paid sick leave will 
not be accrued but will be granted by Client in full via a lump sum dispersal at 
the start of the RetireRehire Worker’s contracted employment period. Client 
will have discretion in determining the type and quantity of paid leave so long 
as this paid leave allotment meets the requirements of the FWHFA.

Client has the right, but not the obligation, to pre-approve any Worker provided by 
ESI to fill a position for which the Client has contracted with ESI to provide. The

lawful reason. Client may recommend that ESI impose discipline upon any Worker 
for any lawful reason, and ESI may, in its own right, impose discipline, up to and 
including dismissal, upon any Worker for any lawful reason. ESI retains the sole right 
to determine if rejection, discipline, or dismissal of a Worker is for a lawful reason.

Client will pay ESI an administrative fee in accordance with the Fee Schedule, 
attached as Exhibit A.  
 
ESI will pay for all associated employer’s payroll liabilities for the Workers.  Payroll 
liabilities include FICA (OASDI/Medicare), federal and state unemployment taxes, 
and workers’ compensation insurance (“Payroll Liabilities”). ESI’s obligation to pay 
all Payroll Liabilities will not change during the Term, even if there are mid-Term 
increases in SUTA or workers’ compensation rates charged for the Workers.  
However, ESI reserves the right to amend Exhibit A and charge Client for increased 
Payroll Liabilities mid-Term only in the event of the following: A) an additional 
category of payroll tax applicable to the Workers is mandated by any federal, state, 
or local government that was unforeseen at the commencement of the Term; or B) 
any federal, state, or local government imposes a material and substantial change to 
the payroll tax structure applicable to the Workers that causes material increased costs 
to ESI that were unforeseen at the commencement of the Term.

workers’ compensation policy shall be endorsed to include the Alternate

services for Client, whether as employees, independent contractors, or 
otherwise. Client agrees to provide workers’ compensation insurance or 
maintain a program of approved self-insurance covering Client’s own 
employees.

13.    ESI’S LIABILITY INSURANCE 
 
 
ESI shall maintain in full force and effect at all times during the Term of this 
Agreement Commercial General Liability (“CGL”) insurance with limits of liability 
of not less than one million dollars ($1,000,000) per occurrence, and if such 
Commercial General Liability insurance contains a general aggregate limit of 
liability, the limit of liability shall be at least two million dollars ($2,000,000). The 
CGL policy shall be written on an occurrence form and shall cover liability arising 
from the independent negligence or other wrongful act, error or omission of ESI and 
its employees that is not the direct consequence of the Services provided by Workers 
under the terms of this Agreement. The Client shall be added as an additional insured 
to the CGL policy, but only to the extent that the covered liability-causing event is 
not related to the Services provided by Workers under the terms of this Agreement. 
 
14.    PATIENT PROTECTION AND AFFORDABLE CARE ACT (PPACA) 
 
 
The parties believe that all Workers are the common law employees of ESI and that 
ESI is an applicable large employer for purposes of compliance with the requirements 
of Code Section 4980H.  Accordingly, ESI offers full-time Workers and their 
“dependents” (as defined in any regulations or other guidance issued under Code 
Section 4980H) the opportunity to enroll in ESI’s “minimum essential coverage” (as 
defined in Code Section 5000A(f) and any regulations or other guidance issued 
thereunder) under an “eligible employer-sponsored plan” (as defined in Code Section 
5000A(f)(2) and any regulations or other guidance issued thereunder) at least once 
per year.  Such coverage will provide “minimum value” (as defined in Code Section 
36B(c)(2)(C)(ii) and any regulations or other guidance issued thereunder). 
 
In addition, ESI will determine full-time employee status for all Workers using the 
“look-back measurement method” (as defined in the regulations or other guidance 
issued under Code Section 4980H) and will conduct the necessary tax reporting as 
required by Code Sections 6055 and 6056, including the timely furnishing of all 
Forms 1095-C to applicable individuals and the timely filing of all Forms 1094-C and 
1095-C with the Internal Revenue Service.

16. 
PROTECTED LEAVES OF ABSENCE  
 
A. 
The Uniformed Services Employment and Reemployment Rights Act of 1994 
(“USERRA”) protects civilian job rights and benefits for military 
servicemembers, veterans, and members of Reserve components. Client agrees 
to provide for any necessary leaves of absence, employment, and/or 
reemployment positions in the event that Workers are called away for or return 
from military service, pursuant to the requirements of USERRA.  
 
B. The Family and Medical Leave Act (“FMLA”) protects job rights, leave, 
benefits, and re-instatement rights of eligible employees after the employee 
has been on approved FMLA leave.  Client agrees to provide for any necessary 
leaves of absence, employment, and/or reemployment positions in the event 
that Workers go on leave or return from leave, pursuant to the requirements of 
the FMLA.

the Worker within 24 hours to Client.

protective equipment as required by federal, state, local law, regulations, 
ordinance, directive, or rule.

to Client on the conditions found at Client’s worksites.  Client will supply 
documentation related to safety activities as prescribed by law (e.g., safety 
meeting, training maintaining OSHA log).  Neither ESI’s insurer nor ESI

operations or premises are in compliance with any laws, regulations, codes, or 
standards.

Board of Supervisors Regular
2. o.        
Meeting Date:
05/14/2024
 
Title:
Delegation Agreement #EV24-0022 Between Arizona Department of Environmental
Quality And Navajo County
Submitted For: Mischa Larisch, Deputy Director of Planning & Development Services 
Submitted By:
Mischa Larisch, Deputy Director of Planning & Development Services
Department:
Public Works
Division:
Planning & Development
Motion before the Board:
Delegation Agreement #EV24-0022, between Arizona Department of Environmental Quality and Navajo
County, specifically Navajo County Department of Public Works Planning and Development
Background:
Arizona Revised Statutes (hereinafter, A.R.S.) § 49-107 authorizes the Director of the Arizona
Department of Environmental Quality (hereinafter, ADEQ) to delegate to a local environmental agency,
county health department or public health services district, any functions, powers, and duties which the
Director believes can be competently, efficiently, and properly performed by the local environmental
agency, county health department or public health services district. Navajo County Planning and
Development has delegated authority for water quality management, specifically for onsite waste water
disposal systems (septic) less than 3000 gallons per day. This is a renewal of the already existing
delegation agreement that cleans up confusion where public health and public works were both listed. 
The agreement should only have public works planning and development listed.
